			THE book of the generation of Jesus Christ, the son of David, the son of Abraham.

			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Βίβλος γενέσεως Ἰησοῦ χριστοῦ υἱοῦ Δαυὶδ υἱοῦ Ἀβραάμ.}

					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ἰακὼβ δὲ ἐγέννησεν τὸν Ἰωσὴφ τὸν ἄνδρα Μαρίας, ἐξ ἧς ἐγεννήθη Ἰησοῦς ὁ λεγόμενος χριστός.}

					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 1:17} % *MATTHEW-1-17 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-1-17}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Notice that this relationship back to David and Abraham is through Joseph's family history, but the narrative doesn't seem to care that Joseph was not the actual father of Christ. Christ is still part of this series of generations going back up to David and Abraham. It's a figurative membership in the lineage that he inherits just by being a part of this particular family; this may be a useful model for thinking about sealings, eternal families, and also the priesthood and the network of relationships we enter into when we make covenants. Compare us being the ``\hyperref[NEWBIRTH-children-christ]{children of Christ}.''
					}
			So all the generations from Abraham to David are fourteen generations; and from David until the carrying away into Babylon are fourteen generations; and from the carrying away into Babylon unto Christ are fourteen generations.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Πᾶσαι οὖν αἱ γενεαὶ ἀπὸ Ἀβραὰμ ἕως Δαυὶδ γενεαὶ δεκατέσσαρες, καὶ ἀπὸ Δαυὶδ ἕως τῆς μετοικεσίας Βαβυλῶνος γενεαὶ δεκατέσσαρες, καὶ ἀπὸ τῆς μετοικεσίας Βαβυλῶνος ἕως τοῦ χριστοῦ γενεαὶ δεκατέσσαρες.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		\paragraph{Matthew 1:18} % *MATTHEW-1-18 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-1-18}

			Now the birth of Jesus Christ was on this wise: When as his mother Mary was espoused to Joseph, before they came together, she was found with child of the Holy Ghost.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Τοῦ δὲ Ἰησοῦ χριστοῦ ἡ γένεσις οὕτως ἦν. μνηστευθείσης τῆς μητρὸς αὐτοῦ Μαρίας τῷ Ἰωσήφ, πρὶν ἢ συνελθεῖν αὐτοὺς εὑρέθη ἐν γαστρὶ ἔχουσα ἐκ πνεύματος ἁγίου.}

					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-1-19}

			Then Joseph her husband, being a just man, and not willing to make her a publick example, was minded to put her away privily.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ἰωσὴφ δὲ ὁ ἀνὴρ αὐτῆς, δίκαιος ὢν καὶ μὴ θέλων αὐτὴν δειγματίσαι, ἐβουλήθη λάθρᾳ ἀπολῦσαι αὐτήν.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 1:20} % *MATTHEW-1-20 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-1-20}

			But while he thought on these things, behold, the angel of the Lord appeared%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					This angel appearing makes me think of instances in the early days of the restoration, when angels had to appear to convince people to do certain things that they might not otherwise have done.
					}
			unto him in a dream, saying, Joseph, thou son of David,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Invoking David's name here is interesting---tapping into the lineage that Joseph knew he was a part of. Perhaps also thinking about David's misdeeds with the wife of Urias?
					}
			fear not to take unto thee Mary thy wife: for that which is conceived in her is of the Holy Ghost.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ταῦτα δὲ αὐτοῦ ἐνθυμηθέντος ἰδοὺ ἄγγελος κυρίου κατ᾽ ὄναρ ἐφάνη αὐτῷ λέγων· Ἰωσὴφ υἱὸς Δαυίδ, μὴ φοβηθῇς παραλαβεῖν Μαρίαν τὴν γυναῖκά σου, τὸ γὰρ ἐν αὐτῇ γεννηθὲν ἐκ πνεύματός ἐστιν ἁγίου·}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 1:21} % *MATTHEW-1-21 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-1-21}

			And she shall bring forth a son, and thou shalt call his name JESUS:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					\he{ye+sU`a}, which itself is a later form of \he{y:hO+sU`a}, ``Jehovah is salvation''; the verb is \he{yA+sa`}, ``to save.''
					}

					}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 2:11} % *MATTHEW-2-11 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-2-11}

			And when they were come into the house, they saw the young child with Mary his mother, and fell down, and worshipped him: and when they had opened their treasures, they presented unto him gifts;
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					ZIBBC for Matthew/Mark/Luke, PDF 67--68: ```Gold' was valued throughout the ancient world as a medium of exchange as well as a precious metal for making jewelry, ornaments, and dining instruments for royalty. `Incense' or frankincense is derived from an amber resin, which produced a sweet odor when burned. It was used as a perfume (Song 3:6; 4:6, 14), but in Israel it was used ceremonially for the only incense permitted on the altar (\hyperref[EXODUS-30-9]{Ex. 30:9}, \hyperref[EXODUS-39-34]{34--38}). `Myrrh' consists of a mixture of resin, gum, and the oil myrrhol and was used in incense (\hyperref[EXODUS-30-23]{Ex. 30:23}), as a perfume for garments or for a lover's couch, as a stimulant (cf. \hyperref[MARK-15-23]{Mark 15:23}), and to pack in the wrappings of the clothing of a deceased person to stifle the smell of the body decaying (cf. \hyperref[JOHN-19-39]{John 19:39}).''
					
					From BIBBC PDF 883: ``Evidently the appearance of a new light in the night sky (a star in the constellation of Israel?) convinced these astrologers that a powerful king had been born in Israel. Consequently, as emissaries of their kings, the magi brought gifts to Herod in Jerusalem, thinking that he must have welcomed a new son into the world. Gold, frankincense  a sacred incense used by priests), and myrrh (perfume used for interment of corpses) were luxurious gifts befitting a king.''
					}%
			gold, and frankincense, and myrrh.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ἐλθόντες εἰς τὴν οἰκίαν εἶδον τὸ παιδίον μετὰ Μαρίας τῆς μητρὸς αὐτοῦ, καὶ πεσόντες προσεκύνησαν αὐτῷ, καὶ ἀνοίξαντες τοὺς θησαυροὺς αὐτῶν προσήνεγκαν αὐτῷ δῶρα, χρυσὸν καὶ λίβανον καὶ σμύρναν.}

			\scriptsize

		\paragraph{Matthew 2:15} % *MATTHEW-2-15 
		% ---

			And was there until the death of Herod: that it might be fulfilled which was spoken of the Lord by the
				\footnote{%

					See \hyperref[HOSEA-11-1]{Hosea 11:1}.
					}%
			prophet, saying, Out of Egypt have I called my son.

			\begin{framed}

			good fruit is hewn down, and cast into the fire.

				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἤδη δὲ ἡ ἀξίνη πρὸς τὴν ῥίζαν τῶν δένδρων κεῖται· πᾶν οὖν δένδρον μὴ ποιοῦν καρπὸν καλὸν ἐκκόπτεται καὶ εἰς πῦρ βάλλεται.}

		\paragraph{Matthew 3:11} % *MATTHEW-3-11 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-3-11}

			I indeed baptize you with water unto repentance: but he that cometh after me is mightier than I, whose shoes I am not worthy to bear:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					All four accounts emphasize that John is below Christ in an important way; that Christ is above him, superior to him, and will do something different and special. Matthew, Mark, and Luke all have \gr{ἱκανός} here, while John has \gr{ἄξιος}.
					}
			he shall baptize you with the Holy Ghost, and with
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[2NEPHI-31-13]{2 Nephi 31:13}. Also, note that there's some not-so-good fire in the next verse.
					}%
			fire:

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ἐγὼ μὲν ὑμᾶς βαπτίζω ἐν ὕδατι εἰς μετάνοιαν· ὁ δὲ ὀπίσω μου ἐρχόμενος ἰσχυρότερός μού ἐστιν, οὗ οὐκ εἰμὶ ἱκανὸς τὰ ὑποδήματα βαστάσαι· αὐτὸς ὑμᾶς βαπτίσει ἐν πνεύματι ἁγίῳ καὶ πυρί·}

					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]

			\end{framed}

		\paragraph{Matthew 3:12} % *MATTHEW-3-12

					\item[SBLG] \gr{βαπτισθεὶς δὲ ὁ Ἰησοῦς εὐθὺς ἀνέβη ἀπὸ τοῦ ὕδατος· καὶ ἰδοὺ ἠνεῴχθησαν οἱ οὐρανοί, καὶ εἶδεν πνεῦμα θεοῦ καταβαῖνον ὡσεὶ περιστερὰν ἐρχόμενον ἐπ᾽ αὐτόν·}

			And lo a voice from heaven, saying,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Notice the difference between the remarks of the voice as given in Matthew, and as given in Mark and Luke: in Matthew the voice says ``This is my son,'' speaking of Christ in the third person, but in Mark and Luke the voice says ``\textit{You} are my son.'' The Mark and Luke accounts are identical, both speaking to Christ. 
					} 
			This is my beloved Son, in whom%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					The Greek text of the Mark and Luke accounts has \gr{ἐν σοὶ} here.
					}

	\subsubsection{Matthew 4} % *MATTHEW-4 
	% -----
		\label{MATTHEW-4}
		
		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 4:1} % *MATTHEW-4-1 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-4-1}

			THEN was Jesus led up of the Spirit into the wilderness to be tempted of the devil.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Τότε ὁ Ἰησοῦς ἀνήχθη εἰς τὴν ἔρημον ὑπὸ τοῦ πνεύματος,} \hyperref[PEIRAZO]{\grl{πειρασθῆναι}} \gr{ὑπὸ τοῦ διαβόλου.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 4:2} % *MATTHEW-4-2 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-4-2}

			And when he had fasted forty days and forty nights,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					On the length of Christ's fast, hunger strikes by protesters routinely last more than forty days, getting up into the fifties and even the sixties in some cases (an Irish hunger striker named Laurence McKeown made it 71 days in 1981, but would have died had his family not intervened). So it seems possible that Christ could have started and ended a forty-day fast by himself, as long as he was drinking water---and see below for more on that. Of those who died in those Irish hunger strikes, all made it more than forty days, with the lowest being 46 and the next-lowest being 59.
				
					There are important differences in the Luke account on two points here---first, Luke clarifies that Christ did not \textit{eat} during the forty days, leaving open the possibility that he was drinking. And Luke also make it seem as though Christ was tempted those entire forty days, not just at the end.
					}
			he was afterward an hungred.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ νηστεύσας ἡμέρας τεσσεράκοντα καὶ νύκτας τεσσεράκοντα ὕστερον ἐπείνασεν.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-4-3}

			And when the tempter came to him, he said, If thou be the Son of God, command that these stones%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Part of John the Baptist's preaching had been to say that God could raise up ``seed'' to Abraham from some stones. I wonder if Christ was aware of that preaching and so might have been thinking of the power to turn stones into what you wanted.
					}
			be made bread.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ προσελθὼν ὁ πειράζων εἶπεν αὐτῷ· Εἰ υἱὸς εἶ τοῦ θεοῦ, εἰπὲ ἵνα οἱ λίθοι οὗτοι ἄρτοι γένωνται.}

					% \item[ASV]

		% ---

			But he answered and said, It is written,%
			\footnote{%
				% \label{}%
				See \hyperref[DEUTERONOMY-8-3]{Deuteronomy 8:3}.
				} 
			Man shall not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceedeth out of the mouth of God.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὁ δὲ ἀποκριθεὶς εἶπεν· Γέγραπται· Οὐκ ἐπ᾽ ἄρτῳ μόνῳ ζήσεται ὁ ἄνθρωπος, ἀλλ᾽ ἐπὶ παντὶ ῥήματι ἐκπορευομένῳ διὰ στόματος θεοῦ.}

					}

		\paragraph{Matthew 4:8} % *MATTHEW-4-8 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-4-8}

			Again, the devil taketh him up into an exceeding high mountain, and sheweth him all the kingdoms of the world,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Definition \#3 for \gr{κόσμος} is ``the sum total of everything here and now, \textit{the world, the (orderly) universe}.'' I think it likely that Satan showed Christ the glory of the kingdoms of the entire universe, not just some view from a tall mountain. That's thinking too small. Christ felt that it was more important to retain his freedom than to receive power over those kingdoms. Note that Luke has \gr{οἰκουμένη} here.
					} 
			and the glory of them;

			\begin{framed}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-4-13}

			And leaving Nazareth, he came and dwelt in Capernaum, which is upon the sea coast, in the borders of Zabulon and Nephthalim:

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ καταλιπὼν τὴν Ναζαρὰ ἐλθὼν κατῴκησεν εἰς Καφαρναοὺμ τὴν παραθαλασσίαν ἐν ὁρίοις Ζαβουλὼν καὶ Νεφθαλίμ·}
					% \item[SPNT]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 5:3} % *MATTHEW-5-3 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-5-3}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					For commentary on the Beatitudes as a whole, see \hyperref[EXSS-NT-BEATITUDE]{here}.
					}%
			Blessed are the poor%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					I connect this poverty to \hyperref[3NEPHI-9-20]{3 Nephi 9:20} and the idea of a ``broken heart.''
					
					WBC 33a 91--92: ``lit. ``the poor in spirit,'' the subject of the first beatitude, refers to the frame of mind characteristic of the literally poor. Thus, by the added ``in spirit,'' Matthew or the tradition before him has not ``spiritualized'' the Lukan (and probably original) form of the beatitude...He too means the literally poor, but he focuses on their psychological condition or frame of mind. The poor are almost always poor in spirit; the poor in spirit are almost always the poor (cf. Broer [71], who notes that the two phrases were synonymous in the Judaism of Jesus' time). In Israel, especially in the post-exilic period, poverty and piety often went together, the poor (Luz refers to the ``déclassé'') having no other recourse than their hope in God. The poor were driven to complete reliance upon God, and the righteous poor were thought especially to be the objects of God's special concern (cf. Pss 9:18; 33[34]:18; 40:18; Isa 57:15; Jas 2:5). The poor were particularly in view in expressions of eschatological hope. In a passage alluded to in Matt 11:5, Isaiah (\hyperref ISAIAH-61-1]{61:1}) writes: [quotes Isaiah 61:1]. This passage is almost certainly the basis for the present beatitude. The good news that has now come to the poor is that the kingdom is ``theirs'' (\gr{αὐτῶν} is in an emphatic position). Thus this opening beatitude points to eschatological fulfillment (cf. the citation of Isa 61:1-2 and the beginning of Jesus' Galilean ministry in Luke 4:18-19). The exact expression ``poor in spirit'' (\he{`nwy rw.h}) is found in the War Scroll from Qumran (1QM 14:7), where the community describes itself, the ``sons of light,'' as those who are poor in spirit. Although membership in the community entailed a voluntary poverty, this reference indicates how the literally poor were identified as the righteous. Zimmerli (19) finds the equivalent concept in the combination of passages in Isaiah referring to the poor (Isa 61:1) and the contrite in spirit (Isa 57:15; 66:2).''
					}
			in spirit:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[DC56-18]{DC 56:18}.
					}
			for theirs is the kingdom of heaven.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 5:13} % *MATTHEW-5-13 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-5-13}

			Ye are the salt of the earth:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					See \hyperref[DC101-39]{DC 101:39--40}.
					}
			but if the salt have lost%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					\gr{μωραίνω}. BDAG 587b: 
						\begin{compactdesc}
							\item[1] \textbf{make foolish, \textit{show to be foolish}}
							\item[2] \textbf{\textit{make tasteless}} passive \textit{become tasteless, insipid}, of salt
						\end{compactdesc}
					}
			his savour, wherewith shall it be salted?%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					IVPBBC NT 56: ``Various scholars have emphasized different uses of salt in antiquity, such as a preservative or an agent regularly added to manure; but the use of salt here is as a flavoring agent: `if salt has become tasteless' (the Greek word can also mean `become foolish,' so it may include a play on words). Although the salt recovered from impure salt substances taken from the Dead Sea could dissolve, leaving only the impurities behind, the point here may be closer to that expressed by a rabbi at the end of the first century. When asked how one could make saltless salt salty again, he replied that one should salt it with the afterbirth of a mule. Being sterile, mules have no afterbirth, and the rabbi was saying that those who ask a stupid question receive a stupid answer. Real salt does not lose its saltiness; but if it did, what would you do to restore its salty flavor---salt it? Unsalty salt was worthless.''
					}
			it is thenceforth good for nothing, but to be cast out, and to be trodden under foot of men.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ὑμεῖς ἐστε τὸ ἅλας τῆς γῆς· ἐὰν δὲ τὸ ἅλας μωρανθῇ, ἐν τίνι ἁλισθήσεται; εἰς οὐδὲν ἰσχύει ἔτι εἰ μὴ βληθὲν ἔξω καταπατεῖσθαι ὑπὸ τῶν ἀνθρώπων.}

					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]

					% \item[NTR2]

					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 5:22} % *MATTHEW-5-22 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-5-22}

			But I say unto you, That whosoever is angry%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					This is a participle, not an adjective; compare how Christ \gr{περιβλεψάμενος αὐτοὺς μετ᾽ ὀργῆς} in \hyperref[MARK-3-5]{Mark 3:5}, and how the Pharisees get after he heals the man's hand in \hyperref[LUKE-6-11]{Luke 6:11}: \gr{αὐτοὶ ἐπλήσθησαν ἀνοίας}. The adjective \gr{ἄνοος} doesn't appear in BDAG, but you can see it's from \gr{α-νους}, and LSJ says it means ``without understanding, silly.''
					}
			with his brother without a cause%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					``Without a cause'' is absent in the critical editions of the Greek, and also in the JST; I wrote a Climbing the Rainbows essay about this phrase, which you can see \href{https://drive.google.com/file/d/18JUkvsQXogUnfXRDGKNx31-bCx2OLJr7/view?usp=share_link}{here}.
					}
			shall be in danger of the judgment:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					It is absolutely crucial to our understanding of this verse that Christ does not say, whoever is angry with his brother has done something \textit{wrong}. What he says is, such a person \gr{ἔνοχος ἔσται τῇ κρίσει}, which in KJV English comes in as ``shall be in danger of the judgment.'' Meaning \#2 of \gr{ἔνοχος} in BDAG (299a) ``\textbf{pert. to being required to give an account for someth. held against one, \textit{liable, answerable, guilty}}''; meaning \#2.a then lists this verse and says, ``w. dat. to denote the tribunal to which one is answerable, \gr{τ. κρίσει}, \gr{τ. συνεδρίῳ}.'' So \gr{τῇ κρίσει} refers to \textit{our judgment}, or the accounting we will give of our actions later on; it isn't that we're in ``danger of the judgment'' of others or even God. So what Christ is saying here is, if you get angry, you better be prepared to explain why.
					}
			and whosoever shall say to his brother, Raca, shall be in danger of the council: but whosoever shall say, Thou fool, shall be in danger of hell fire.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἐγὼ δὲ λέγω ὑμῖν ὅτι πᾶς ὁ ὀργιζόμενος τῷ ἀδελφῷ αὐτοῦ ἔνοχος ἔσται τῇ} \hyperref[KRISIS]{\grl{κρίσει}}\gr{· ὃς δ᾽ ἂν εἴπῃ τῷ ἀδελφῷ αὐτοῦ· Ῥακά, ἔνοχος ἔσται τῷ συνεδρίῳ· ὃς δ᾽ ἂν εἴπῃ· Μωρέ, ἔνοχος ἔσται εἰς τὴν γέενναν τοῦ πυρός.}
					% \item[SPNT]

					% \item[ASV]

					% \item[NTR2]

					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---

			Ye have heard that it was said by them of old time, Thou shalt not commit adultery:

			\begin{framed}

					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ἠκούσατε ὅτι ἐρρέθη· Οὐ μοιχεύσεις.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 5:28} % *MATTHEW-5-28 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-5-28}

			But I say unto you, That whosoever looketh on a woman to lust after her hath committed adultery%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{LCHRIST-042-adultery}%
					The Greek verb for ``commit adultery'' here is \gr{μοιχεύω}. At \hyperref[MOICHEUO]{\grl{μοιχεύω}}, BDAG says that this is a use of the term which applies only to a man or to men in general (meaning \#1.b.$\beta$). See \hyperref[EPITHUMEO]{\grl{ἐπιθυμέω}}, here translated as ``lust,'' for the definitions of that word; the one BDAG assigns to this verse is \#2, ``to have sexual interest in someone, \textit{desire}.'' See \hyperref[ROMANS-1-24]{Romans 1:24}, \hyperref[GALATIANS-5-16]{Galatians 5:16} (which may help us understand what it means \gr{πρὸς τὸ ἐπιθυμῆσαι αὐτὴν}),
					
					We need a way of understanding this verse that does not make it sinful to appreciate the beauty of someone else's body while leaving it there and not letting it go any further. For what I think is the proper way to experience beautiful women as a person who has made temple covenants, see \hyperref[TTW-WOMEN-OTHERS-0001]{Deida's thoughts}---the whole thing, but especially the last paragraph.
				}
			with her already
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[1NEPHI-17-44]{1 Nephi 17:44}, ``murderers in your hearts.''
					}%
			in his heart.%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[DC63-16]{DC 63:16},
					}

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἐγὼ δὲ λέγω ὑμῖν ὅτι πᾶς ὁ βλέπων γυναῖκα πρὸς τὸ ἐπιθυμῆσαι αὐτὴν ἤδη ἐμοίχευσεν αὐτὴν ἐν τῇ καρδίᾳ αὐτοῦ.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 5:46} % *MATTHEW-5-46 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-5-46}

			For if ye love them which love you, what reward%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Here and into the next chapter, where we get \gr{μισθὸν} as the ``reward,'' remember that \hyperref[LUKE-6-32]{Luke 6:32--34} uses \gr{χάρις}---I think as a way to explain what the actual reward is.
					}
			have ye? do not even the publicans the same?

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}

			And if ye salute your brethren only, what do ye more than others? do not even the publicans so?

				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ἐὰν ἀσπάσησθε τοὺς ἀδελφοὺς ὑμῶν μόνον, τί περισσὸν ποιεῖτε; οὐχὶ καὶ οἱ ἐθνικοὶ τὸ αὐτὸ ποιοῦσιν;}

		% --
		\paragraph{Matthew 5:48}
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-5-48}
			
			Be ye therefore perfect, even as your Father which is in heaven is perfect.
			
			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ἔσεσθε οὖν ὑμεῖς τέλειοι ὡς ὁ πατὴρ ὑμῶν ὁ οὐράνιος} \hyperref[TELEIOS]{\grl{τέλειός}} \gr{ἐστιν.}
					\item[AB 71] Be true, just as your heavenly Father is true.\footnotemark
					\item[ASV] Ye therefore shall be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect.
					\item[ESV] You therefore must be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect.
					\item[NASB] ``Therefore you are to be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect.
					\item[NIV] Be perfect, therefore, as your heavenly Father is perfect.
					\item[NRSV] Be perfect, therefore, as your heavenly Father is perfect.
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}
				\footnotetext{%
					\label{MATTHEW-5-48-perfect}%
					See the alternative translations below; most render \gr{τέλειός} as ``perfect.'' The exception is AB. AB 71--72 includes this note, on using ``true'' here: ``A rabbinic commentary...quoting a first-century a.d. authority, paraphrases this as: `Be like him. As he is gracious and merciful, so be you gracious and merciful.' The Greek word \gr{τέλειός} in this context does not refer to moral perfection, but `truth, sincerity' (cf. \hyperref[DEUTERONOMY-18-13]{Deuteronomy 18:13}). In this sense, the Greek word is used in the LXX about Noah (\hyperref[GENESIS-6-9]{Genesis 6:9}) and Job (\hyperref[JOB-1-1]{Job 1:1}). The Greek word in the LXX is linked with \he{t*Amiym}, and the meaning of the Canaanite- Hebrew word \he{t*Am}, `true,' is the same in both pagan and biblical literature. There are links in Hebrew between \he{t*Amiym} and \he{'E:mEt} (truth), and also in the LXX with the Gr. \gr{ἀληθινός}, the `true' man. It does not have here the later Greek meaning of being `totally free of imperfection,' which is the meaning found in both the KJ and RSV.''
					
					Not sure what to think about this note; I would need to read more about it, but it doesn't fit well with any of the definitions in any of the lexicons for \hyperref[TELEIOS]{\grl{τέλειός}}. There could be some theological issue at stake that I don't see, but it's a very unusual choice.
					}

	% -----
	\subsubsection{Matthew 6} % *MATTHEW-6 
	% -----
		\label{MATTHEW-6}
		
		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 6:1} % *MATTHEW-6-1 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-6-1}

			TAKE heed that ye do not your alms%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					\gr{δικαιοσύνην} in SBLG. I think this is a case where the KJV draws from a different manuscript tradition---my blue Greek NT lists \gr{ελεημοσυνην} as an alternate reading here, but the red one doesn't even list the variant, and MTCGNT doesn't comment on it at all. So it must be pretty clear that the true reading is \gr{δικαιοσύνην}, which I find a lot more interesting than \gr{ελεημοσυνην}. To me this makes a significant contribution to our understanding of the principle. Note, though, that you do get a form of \gr{ἐλεημοσύνη} in the next verse.
					}
			before men, to be seen of them: otherwise ye have no reward%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					When it's talking about being ``rewarded'' here, I want to connect the reward, here \gr{μισθὸν}, with the times that \gr{χάρις} appears as the reward in \hyperref[LUKE-6-32]{Luke 6:32--34}; from there you can link to \hyperref[DC93]{DC 93} and other places in order to talk about progression.
					}
			of your Father which is in heaven.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Προσέχετε δὲ τὴν} \hyperref[DIKAIOSUNE]{\grl{δικαιοσύνην}} \gr{ὑμῶν μὴ ποιεῖν ἔμπροσθεν τῶν ἀνθρώπων πρὸς τὸ θεαθῆναι αὐτοῖς· εἰ δὲ μή γε, μισθὸν οὐκ ἔχετε παρὰ τῷ πατρὶ ὑμῶν τῷ ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		\paragraph{Matthew 6:7} % *MATTHEW-6-7 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-6-7}

			But when ye pray, use not vain repetitions,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					``Use vain repetitions'' is the unusual verb \gr{βατταλογέω}, which appears in the NT only in this verse (and in Codex Bezae at \hyperref[LUKE-11-2]{Luke 11:2}. BDAG 151a: ``onomatopoetic word; \textbf{to speak in a way that images the kind of speech pattern of one who stammers, \textit{use the same words again and again, speak without thinking}} (explained by \gr{πολυλογία}) Mt 6:7; Lk 11:2 D. Except for writers dependent on the NT the word has been found only in Vi. Aesopi W 109, where Perry notes the v.l. \gr{βατολογέω} for \gr{βαττολογέω} (it is missing in the corresp. place ed. Eberhard I c. 26 p. 289, 9. But Vi. Aesopi G 50 P. has the noun \gr{βαττολογία}=foolish talk, but in a different context), and in Simplicius (c. 530 a.d.), Comm. in Epict. p. 91, 23 in the spelling \gr{βαττολογέω}=`prate'. It is perh. a hybrid form, rendering Aram. \he{'mr b.tlh'}=`talk idly'.''
					}
			as the heathen do: for they think that they shall be heard for their
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					\gr{πολυλογία}. BDAG 751b: ``\textbf{speech of tedious length, \textit{much speaking, wordiness, long-windedness}}  \gr{ἐν τῇ π. αὐτῶν} \textit{with their many words} Mt 6:7; Lk 11:2 D.'' The ``D'' after the Luke verse means that it's talking about the Codex Bezae, a different manuscript; this Greek word isn't actually in \hyperref[LUKE-11-2]{Luke 11:2}.
					}%
			much speaking.

			\begin{framed}

		\paragraph{Matthew 6:16} % *MATTHEW-6-16

			Moreover when ye fast, be not, as the hypocrites, of a sad countenance: for they disfigure their faces, that they may appear unto men to fast. Verily I say unto you, They have their reward.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ὅταν δὲ νηστεύητε, μὴ γίνεσθε ὡς οἱ ὑποκριταὶ σκυθρωποί, ἀφανίζουσιν γὰρ τὰ πρόσωπα αὐτῶν ὅπως φανῶσιν τοῖς ἀνθρώποις νηστεύοντες· ἀμὴν λέγω ὑμῖν, ἀπέχουσιν τὸν μισθὸν αὐτῶν.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 6:17} % *MATTHEW-6-17 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-6-17}

			But thou, when thou fastest,
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					The purpose of anointing your head and washing your face is so that it isn't apparent to others that you are fasting. IVPBBC NT 61: ``Jewish people conjoined fasting with mourning, repentance, or sometimes prayer; most fasts ran from sundown to sundown. During at least the dry seasons, many of the most pious people fasted (without water, though this was unhealthy) two particular days a week. This fasting was considered meritorious, although ascetic fasting (e.g., fasting only to `beat down the flesh') was forbidden. Jewish fasting required abstinence not only from food but also from other pleasures, which would include the usual practice of anointing one's head with oil to prevent dry skin; avoiding all these practices made fasting obvious. (Greeks oiled their bodies before exercise and then used a metal utensil called a strigil to scrape off the sweaty dirt accumulated on the oil. But Jews did not practice this custom, and it is not in view here in Mt 6.) God had never settled for outward fasting only (\hyperref[ISAIAH-58-3]{Is 58:3--12}; \hyperref[JEREMIAH-36-9]{Jer 36:9}).''
					}%
			anoint thine head, and wash thy face;

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{σὺ δὲ νηστεύων ἄλειψαί σου τὴν κεφαλὴν καὶ τὸ πρόσωπόν σου νίψαι,}

				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 7:1} % *MATTHEW-7-1 
		% ---

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Μὴ} \hyperref[KRINO]{\grl{κρίνετε}}, \gr{ἵνα μὴ} \hyperref[KRINO]{\grl{κριθῆτε}}\gr{·}
					% \item[SPNT]

					% \item[NTR1]

			Thou hypocrite, first cast out the beam out of thine own eye; and then shalt thou see clearly%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					The implication here is that the beam blocks you from seeing clearly---with it in, you don't see right.
					} 
			to cast out the mote out of thy brother's eye.

					\item[SBLG] \gr{Μὴ δῶτε τὸ ἅγιον τοῖς κυσίν, μηδὲ βάλητε τοὺς μαργαρίτας ὑμῶν ἔμπροσθεν τῶν χοίρων, μήποτε καταπατήσουσιν αὐτοὺς ἐν τοῖς ποσὶν αὐτῶν καὶ στραφέντες ῥήξωσιν ὑμᾶς.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 7:7} % *MATTHEW-7-7 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-7-7}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					All of these imperatives are present forms, which means they can have progressive aspect---you could bring this out more forcefully in this way: ``Be asking, and it shall be given to you; be seeking, and you shall find; be knocking, and it shall be opened to you.'' If we desire something, we have to \textit{be asking for it} all the time in our \textit{actions}.
					}%
			Ask, and it shall be given you;%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Notice also that all the imperatives and pronouns are plural in this verse.
					}
			seek, and ye shall find; knock, and it shall be opened unto you:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					An extremely powerful promise. Compare \hyperref[DC6-5]{DC 6:5--6} (notice that verse 6 there may shed some light on a way on which we ask---we ask, ``What should I do?''), 
					}

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Αἰτεῖτε, καὶ δοθήσεται ὑμῖν· ζητεῖτε, καὶ εὑρήσετε· κρούετε, καὶ ἀνοιγήσεται ὑμῖν.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 7:8} % *MATTHEW-7-8 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-7-8}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Present participles here, again with progressive aspect.
					}%
			For every one that asketh receiveth;%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					It's interesting that there's a change in the sequence here. There are six participles and verbs in this verse, and five of them also appear in the previous verse, but the second one is different---based on the pattern you would expect ``everyone one that asketh will have it given,'' or something like that, but it just uses \gr{λαμβάνει} instead of a form of \gr{δίδωμι}. The emphasis is on the action of reception by the person who knocks, so maybe the suggestion is that it's only the person who asks that is in a position or mindset to receive.
					}
			and he that seeketh findeth; and to him that knocketh it shall be opened.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{πᾶς γὰρ ὁ αἰτῶν λαμβάνει καὶ ὁ ζητῶν εὑρίσκει καὶ τῷ κρούοντι ἀνοιγήσεται.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		\paragraph{Matthew 7:12} % *MATTHEW-7-12 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-7-12}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					The ``golden rule.'' Sometimes students in my Kant class meetings will notice that the categorical imperative seems to amount to this rule. See \hyperref[ALMA-41-12]{Alma 41:12--15} and \hyperref[DC88-40]{DC 88:40} for ways to go beyond a simplistic interpretation of the rule.
					}%
			Therefore all things whatsoever ye would that men should do to you, do ye even so to them: for this is the law and the prophets.

			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Πάντα οὖν ὅσα ἐὰν θέλητε ἵνα ποιῶσιν ὑμῖν οἱ ἄνθρωποι, οὕτως καὶ ὑμεῖς ποιεῖτε αὐτοῖς· οὗτος γάρ ἐστιν ὁ νόμος καὶ οἱ προφῆται.}

		\paragraph{Matthew 7:13} % *MATTHEW-7-13 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-7-13}

			Enter ye in at the strait gate:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					\gr{διὰ τῆς στενῆς πύλης}. \gr{στενή} means ``narrow'' (BDAG 838a); ``strait'' also means ``narrow; close; not broad'' (\#1); ``strict; rigorous'' (\#3); ``difficult, distressful'' (\#4); ``straight; not crooked'' (\#5, all from 1828).
					}
			for wide is the gate,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					See \hyperref[DC43-7]{DC 43:7} and notes there.
					}
			and broad is the way, that leadeth to destruction, and many there be which go in thereat:

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Εἰσέλθατε διὰ τῆς στενῆς πύλης· ὅτι πλατεῖα ἡ πύλη καὶ εὐρύχωρος ἡ ὁδὸς ἡ ἀπάγουσα εἰς τὴν ἀπώλειαν, καὶ πολλοί εἰσιν οἱ εἰσερχόμενοι δι᾽ αὐτῆς·}

					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 7:17} % *MATTHEW-7-17 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-7-17}

			Even so every good%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					First of all, notice that the adjective \gr{ἀγαθὸν} is not in attributive position; so it could be like, ``every tree, when it is good, produces good fruits.'' But also notice that the two uses of ``good'' in this first part of the verse are different Greek words. The first is \hyperref[AGATHOS]{\grl{ἀγαθὸν}} and the second is \hyperref[KALOS]{\grl{καλοὺς}}, referring to the fruit. The distinction is maintained in the next verse as well. Compare \hyperref[MATTHEW-12-34]{Matthew 12:34--35} and \hyperref[LUKE-6-43]{Luke 6:43--45}, which have different uses.
					}
			tree bringeth forth good fruit; but a corrupt tree bringeth forth evil fruit.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{οὕτως πᾶν δένδρον} \hyperref[AGATHOS]{\grl{ἀγαθὸν}} \gr{καρποὺς} \hyperref[KALOS]{\grl{καλοὺς}} \gr{ποιεῖ, τὸ δὲ σαπρὸν δένδρον καρποὺς πονηροὺς ποιεῖ·}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]

					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---

			Wherefore by their fruits ye shall know them.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἄρα γε ἀπὸ τῶν καρπῶν αὐτῶν ἐπιγνώσεσθε αὐτούς.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-7-24}

			Therefore whosoever heareth these sayings of mine, and doeth them, I will liken him unto a wise man, which built his house upon a
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[MATTHEW-16-18]{Matthew 16:18ff}.
					}%
			rock:

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Πᾶς οὖν ὅστις ἀκούει μου τοὺς λόγους τούτους καὶ ποιεῖ αὐτούς, ὁμοιωθήσεται ἀνδρὶ φρονίμῳ, ὅστις ᾠκοδόμησεν αὐτοῦ τὴν οἰκίαν ἐπὶ τὴν πέτραν.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 7:25} % *MATTHEW-7-25 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-7-25}

			And the rain descended, and the floods came, and the winds blew, and beat upon that house; and it fell not:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					There's a subtle pun/connection here in Greek---the ``beat upon'' bit is a form of \gr{προσπίπτω}, while ``it fell not'' is \gr{οὐκ ἔπεσεν}, which is another form of \gr{πίπτω}. \gr{προσπίπτω} is an interesting word---BDAG 784a gives the first definition as ``\textbf{to prostrate oneself before someone, \textit{fall down before/at the feet of}} freq. in the gesture of a suppliant,'' and then the second definition as ``\textbf{to move with force against something, \textit{fall upon, strike against}}.'' This makes me think of Christ is the one whom the elements obey, as in \hyperref[MATTHEW-8-27]{Matthew 8:27} (see also \hyperref[MARK-4-41]{Mark 4:41} and \hyperref[ABRAHAM-2-7]{Abraham 2:7}), and when we are built on him, we are the house that the winds and the elements \gr{προσπίπτω} in the sense of ``beat upon,'' but also \gr{προσπίπτω} in the sense of how they will eventually fall down at our feet to obey us (\hyperref[JACOB-4-6]{Jacob 4:6}). This is because we become the children of Christ, or in other words, we enter an order of the priesthood in which Christ is the figure through whom the power flows (\hyperref[DC107-3]{DC 107:3}). I think this story Christ is telling here about building upon the rock can be taken as an illustration of our covenant relationship with Christ and how we receive priesthood power through him, and will eventually have the power to do what he can do.
					}
			for it was founded upon a rock.%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[HELAMAN-5-12]{Helaman 5:12}; 
					}

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ κατέβη ἡ βροχὴ καὶ ἦλθον οἱ ποταμοὶ καὶ ἔπνευσαν οἱ ἄνεμοι καὶ προσέπεσαν τῇ οἰκίᾳ ἐκείνῃ, καὶ οὐκ ἔπεσεν, τεθεμελίωτο γὰρ ἐπὶ τὴν πέτραν.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

			And he touched her hand,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					This is an interesting story to follow after the centurion, because in that story, Christ heals from a distance, with no physical touch---but in this one there is physical touch. So it can happen both ways.

		\paragraph{Matthew 8:20} % *MATTHEW-8-20 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-8-20}

			And Jesus saith unto him, The foxes have holes,%
				\footnote{%
					\label{MATTHEW-8-20-foxes}%
					From JSIM 57--58: ``The evening darkened the clouds lowered, and soon abundance of rain fell which, tho' refreshing to the crops \& grass was not very pleasant to us `sub tegmini fagi,' having only the branches and leaves of a large tree to shelter us, this damped our energy a little, but the consideration that our elder brother Jesus Christ had passed through such storms---had said ``The Foxes have holes, the birds of the air have nests, but the son of man has not where to lay his head'' measurable comforted us. We like him have the ground only for our beds, the wide space above for our chambers, and saddles \&c. for our pillows, we had more than Jacob, who had only a rock to lay his head upon then beheld he the opening heavens, Angel's visits, then `not few \& far between' why so now? these reflections induce contentment, backed by the abiding belief that we are doing our Father's will, the rain ceased, and the heavens cleared off, and soon we sank among the Indians to sleep unguarded \& enjoyed a sweet rest, 3 Indians slept on my right hand, \& about 3 $\frac{1}{2}$ a.m. I was awoke by their bringing wood and making fires.''
					}
			and the birds of the air have nests; but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ λέγει αὐτῷ ὁ Ἰησοῦς· Αἱ ἀλώπεκες φωλεοὺς ἔχουσιν καὶ τὰ πετεινὰ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ κατασκηνώσεις, ὁ δὲ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου οὐκ ἔχει ποῦ τὴν κεφαλὴν κλίνῃ.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 8:32} % *MATTHEW-8-32 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-8-32}

			And he said unto them, Go. And when they were come out, they went into the herd of swine: and, behold,
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					What explains why they ran the swine into the sea? The animals died after, so why would the spirits have wanted this to happen? I'm not really sure, but one possible explanation is that the spirits who took control of the pigs didn't know how to control a pig body, or they weren't as familiar with how they pig bodies worked, and so they were only able to do some confused action on a slope, which apparently led them to fall into the water. I'm not sure if this is right.
					}%
			the whole herd of swine ran violently down a steep place into the sea, and perished in the waters.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ εἶπεν αὐτοῖς· Ὑπάγετε. οἱ δὲ ἐξελθόντες ἀπῆλθον εἰς τοὺς χοίρους· καὶ ἰδοὺ ὥρμησεν πᾶσα ἡ ἀγέλη κατὰ τοῦ κρημνοῦ εἰς τὴν θάλασσαν, καὶ ἀπέθανον ἐν τοῖς ὕδασιν.}

				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-9-12}

			But when Jesus heard that, he said unto them, They that be whole%

					% \label{}%
					A participle, \gr{ἰσχύοντες}. \gr{ἰσχύω} can mean ``physically healthy'' but it can also mean ``have power, be able.'' Christ's remark here reminds me of \hyperref[DC84-106]{DC 84:106}.
					}
			need not a physician, but they that are sick.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὁ δὲ ἀκούσας εἶπεν· Οὐ χρείαν ἔχουσιν οἱ ἰσχύοντες ἰατροῦ ἀλλὰ οἱ κακῶς ἔχοντες.}

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize

		\paragraph{Matthew 9:16} % *MATTHEW-9-16 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-9-16}

			No man putteth a piece of new cloth unto an old garment, for that which is put in to fill it up taketh from the garment, and the rent is made worse.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{οὐδεὶς δὲ ἐπιβάλλει ἐπίβλημα ῥάκους ἀγνάφου ἐπὶ ἱματίῳ παλαιῷ· αἴρει γὰρ τὸ πλήρωμα αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ τοῦ ἱματίου, καὶ χεῖρον σχίσμα γίνεται.}

					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]

					% \item[NTXXX*]

					% \item[NTXXXXX*]

				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 9:17} % *MATTHEW-9-17 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-9-17}

			Neither do men put new wine into old bottles: else the bottles break, and the wine runneth out, and the bottles perish:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					See also \hyperref[MARK-2-21]{Mark 2:21--22} and \hyperref[LUKE-5-36]{Luke 5:36--38}. ZIBBC NT 01 605--606: ``The new stronger fabric of a patch will tear away from the old, weaker fabric when the garment is washed. Old wineskins already stretched to their limits will burst their seams when the new wine continues to ferment and emit gas. Combining new with old will result in torn garments, spilled wine, and ruined wineskins. The point is that the old---the old forms of Judaism---is incompatible with the new, not because the old is outmoded, but because the new packs such power that the old cannot contain it.''
					}
			but they put new wine into new bottles, and both are preserved.%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					I think we have to think of ourselves as bottles here---are we a new bottle, such that we can handle the new, or are we an old bottle, only able to be filled with what is old? I think the \hyperref[NEWBIRTH]{new birth} can help us to develop this flexibility of thought, a childlike attitude toward the world where we recognize we don't know everything and are constantly trying to learn and constantly updating our model of the real.
					}

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{οὐδὲ βάλλουσιν οἶνον νέον εἰς ἀσκοὺς παλαιούς· εἰ δὲ μή γε, ῥήγνυνται οἱ ἀσκοί, καὶ ὁ οἶνος ἐκχεῖται καὶ οἱ ἀσκοὶ ἀπόλλυνται· ἀλλὰ βάλλουσιν οἶνον νέον εἰς ἀσκοὺς καινούς, καὶ ἀμφότεροι συντηροῦνται.}

	\subsubsection{Matthew 10} % *MATTHEW-10 
	% -----
		\label{MATTHEW-10}
		
		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:1} % *MATTHEW-10-1 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-1}

			AND when he had called unto him his twelve disciples, he gave them power%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Note that it's \gr{ἐξουσίαν} here, but in \hyperref[LUKE-9-1]{Luke 9:1} it's ``power and authority'' (\gr{δύναμιν καὶ ἐξουσίαν}). This is a good example of how the KJV isn't consistent with the way it handles \gr{δύναμις} and \gr{ἐξουσία} even in contexts where it's clear what we are talking about.
					}
			against unclean spirits, to cast them out, and to heal all manner of sickness and all manner of disease.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Καὶ προσκαλεσάμενος τοὺς δώδεκα μαθητὰς αὐτοῦ ἔδωκεν αὐτοῖς} \hyperref[EXOUSIA]{\grl{ἐξουσίαν}} \gr{πνευμάτων ἀκαθάρτων ὥστε ἐκβάλλειν αὐτὰ καὶ θεραπεύειν πᾶσαν νόσον καὶ πᾶσαν μαλακίαν.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:2} % *MATTHEW-10-2 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-2}

			Now the names of the twelve apostles are these; The first, Simon, who is called Peter, and Andrew his brother; James the son of Zebedee, and John his brother;

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{τῶν δὲ δώδεκα ἀποστόλων τὰ ὀνόματά ἐστιν ταῦτα· πρῶτος Σίμων ὁ λεγόμενος Πέτρος καὶ Ἀνδρέας ὁ ἀδελφὸς αὐτοῦ, Ἰάκωβος ὁ τοῦ Ζεβεδαίου καὶ Ἰωάννης ὁ ἀδελφὸς αὐτοῦ,}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]

					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]

				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---

			Philip, and Bartholomew; Thomas, and Matthew the publican; James the son of Alphæus, and Lebbæus, whose surname was Thaddæus;

			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Φίλιππος καὶ Βαρθολομαῖος, Θωμᾶς καὶ Μαθθαῖος ὁ τελώνης, Ἰάκωβος ὁ τοῦ Ἁλφαίου καὶ Θαδδαῖος,}

		\paragraph{Matthew 10:5} % *MATTHEW-10-5 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-5}

			These twelve Jesus sent forth, and commanded them, saying, Go not into the way of the Gentiles, and into any city of the Samaritans enter ye not:

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Τούτους τοὺς δώδεκα ἀπέστειλεν ὁ Ἰησοῦς παραγγείλας αὐτοῖς λέγων· Εἰς ὁδὸν ἐθνῶν μὴ ἀπέλθητε καὶ εἰς πόλιν Σαμαριτῶν μὴ εἰσέλθητε·}

					\item[SBLG] \gr{πορεύεσθε δὲ μᾶλλον πρὸς τὰ πρόβατα τὰ ἀπολωλότα οἴκου Ἰσραήλ.}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-7}

			And as ye go, preach, saying, The kingdom of heaven is at hand.

			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{πορευόμενοι δὲ κηρύσσετε λέγοντες ὅτι Ἤγγικεν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν.}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-8}

			Heal the sick, cleanse the lepers, raise the dead, cast out devils: freely ye have received, freely give.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἀσθενοῦντας θεραπεύετε, νεκροὺς ἐγείρετε, λεπροὺς καθαρίζετε, δαιμόνια ἐκβάλλετε· δωρεὰν ἐλάβετε, δωρεὰν δότε.}

			Provide neither gold, nor silver, nor brass in your purses,

				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{μὴ κτήσησθε χρυσὸν μηδὲ ἄργυρον μηδὲ χαλκὸν εἰς τὰς ζώνας ὑμῶν,}

		\paragraph{Matthew 10:10} % *MATTHEW-10-10 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-10}

			Nor scrip for your journey, neither two coats, neither shoes, nor yet staves: for the workman is worthy of his meat.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{μὴ πήραν εἰς ὁδὸν μηδὲ δύο χιτῶνας μηδὲ ὑποδήματα μηδὲ ῥάβδον· ἄξιος γὰρ ὁ ἐργάτης τῆς τροφῆς αὐτοῦ.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:11} % *MATTHEW-10-11 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-11}

			And into whatsoever city or town ye shall enter, enquire who in it is worthy; and there abide till ye go thence.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{εἰς ἣν δ᾽ ἂν πόλιν ἢ κώμην εἰσέλθητε, ἐξετάσατε τίς ἐν αὐτῇ ἄξιός ἐστιν· κἀκεῖ μείνατε ἕως ἂν ἐξέλθητε.}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-12}

			And when ye come into an house, salute it.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{εἰσερχόμενοι δὲ εἰς τὴν οἰκίαν ἀσπάσασθε αὐτήν·}

					% \item[BSV]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:13} % *MATTHEW-10-13 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-13}

			And if the house be worthy, let your peace come upon it: but if it be not worthy, let your peace return to you.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ἐὰν μὲν ᾖ ἡ οἰκία ἀξία, ἐλθάτω ἡ εἰρήνη ὑμῶν ἐπ᾽ αὐτήν· ἐὰν δὲ μὴ ᾖ ἀξία, ἡ εἰρήνη ὑμῶν πρὸς ὑμᾶς ἐπιστραφήτω.}

					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:14} % *MATTHEW-10-14 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-14}

			And whosoever shall not receive you, nor hear your words, when ye depart out of that house or city, shake off the dust of your feet.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ὃς ἂν μὴ δέξηται ὑμᾶς μηδὲ ἀκούσῃ τοὺς λόγους ὑμῶν, ἐξερχόμενοι ἔξω τῆς οἰκίας ἢ τῆς πόλεως ἐκείνης ἐκτινάξατε τὸν κονιορτὸν τῶν ποδῶν ὑμῶν.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:15} % *MATTHEW-10-15 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-15}

			Verily I say unto you, It shall be more tolerable for the land of Sodom and Gomorrha in the day of judgment, than for that city.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἀμὴν λέγω ὑμῖν, ἀνεκτότερον ἔσται γῇ Σοδόμων καὶ Γομόρρων ἐν ἡμέρᾳ κρίσεως ἢ τῇ πόλει ἐκείνῃ.}

					% \item[ASV]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:16} % *MATTHEW-10-16 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-16}

			Behold, I send you forth as sheep in the midst of wolves: be ye therefore
			\footnote{%
				% \label{}%
				For ``wise''/``harmless,'' see note at \hyperref[ALMA-18-22]{Alma 18:22}.
				}%
			wise as serpents, and harmless as doves.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ ἀποστέλλω ὑμᾶς ὡς πρόβατα ἐν μέσῳ λύκων· γίνεσθε οὖν φρόνιμοι ὡς οἱ ὄφεις καὶ ἀκέραιοι ὡς αἱ περιστεραί.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:17} % *MATTHEW-10-17 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-17}

			But beware of men: for they will deliver you up to the councils, and they will scourge you in their synagogues;

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{προσέχετε δὲ ἀπὸ τῶν ἀνθρώπων· παραδώσουσιν γὰρ ὑμᾶς εἰς συνέδρια, καὶ ἐν ταῖς συναγωγαῖς αὐτῶν μαστιγώσουσιν ὑμᾶς·}

					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:18} % *MATTHEW-10-18 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-18}

			And ye shall be brought before governors and kings for my sake, for a testimony against them and the Gentiles.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ἐπὶ ἡγεμόνας δὲ καὶ βασιλεῖς ἀχθήσεσθε ἕνεκεν ἐμοῦ εἰς μαρτύριον αὐτοῖς καὶ τοῖς ἔθνεσιν.}

					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:19} % *MATTHEW-10-19 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-19}

			But when they deliver you up, take no thought how or what ye shall speak: for it shall be given you in that same hour what ye shall speak.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὅταν δὲ παραδῶσιν ὑμᾶς, μὴ μεριμνήσητε πῶς ἢ τί λαλήσητε· δοθήσεται γὰρ ὑμῖν ἐν ἐκείνῃ τῇ ὥρᾳ τί λαλήσητε·}

					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:20} % *MATTHEW-10-20 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-20}

			For it is not ye that speak, but the Spirit of your Father which speaketh in you.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{οὐ γὰρ ὑμεῖς ἐστε οἱ λαλοῦντες ἀλλὰ τὸ πνεῦμα τοῦ πατρὸς ὑμῶν τὸ λαλοῦν ἐν ὑμῖν.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:21} % *MATTHEW-10-21 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-21}

			And the brother shall deliver up the brother to death, and the father the child: and the children shall rise up against their parents, and cause them to be put to death.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{παραδώσει δὲ ἀδελφὸς ἀδελφὸν εἰς θάνατον καὶ πατὴρ τέκνον, καὶ ἐπαναστήσονται τέκνα ἐπὶ γονεῖς καὶ θανατώσουσιν αὐτούς.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:22} % *MATTHEW-10-22 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-22}

			And ye shall be hated of all men for my name's sake: but he that endureth to the end shall be saved.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ἔσεσθε μισούμενοι ὑπὸ πάντων διὰ τὸ ὄνομά μου· ὁ δὲ ὑπομείνας εἰς τέλος οὗτος σωθήσεται.}
					% \item[SPNT]

		% ---

			It is enough for the disciple that he be as his master, and the servant as his lord. If they have called the master of the house Beelzebub, how much more shall they call them of his household?

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἀρκετὸν τῷ μαθητῇ ἵνα γένηται ὡς ὁ διδάσκαλος αὐτοῦ, καὶ ὁ δοῦλος ὡς ὁ κύριος αὐτοῦ. εἰ τὸν οἰκοδεσπότην Βεελζεβοὺλ ἐπεκάλεσαν, πόσῳ μᾶλλον τοὺς οἰκιακοὺς αὐτοῦ.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:34} % *MATTHEW-10-34 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-34}

			Think not that I am come to send%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Both ``send''s in this verse are aorist infinitives of \gr{βάλλω}. Definition \#3 in BDAG (143a) for that verb is ``\textbf{to put or place something in a location, \textit{put, place, apply, lay, bring}}.''
					}
			peace%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare to \hyperref[JOHN-14-27]{John 14:27}.
					} 
			on earth: I came not to send peace, but a sword.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Μὴ νομίσητε ὅτι ἦλθον βαλεῖν εἰρήνην ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν· οὐκ ἦλθον βαλεῖν εἰρήνην ἀλλὰ μάχαιραν.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]

		\paragraph{Matthew 10:35} % *MATTHEW-10-35 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-35}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare this verse to \hyperref[MICAH-7-6]{Micah 7:6}.
					}%
			For I am come to set a man at variance%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					I don't know whether the ``at variance'' is just a part of the translation of \gr{διχάζω} (BDAG 224b, ``\textbf{\textit{to divide in two}}'') or whether there are other manuscripts that actually have more support for that phrase; the apparatus of the SBLG doesn't seem to have anything about this. 
					}
			against his father, and the daughter against her mother, and the daughter in law against her mother in law.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἦλθον γὰρ διχάσαι ἄνθρωπον κατὰ τοῦ πατρὸς αὐτοῦ καὶ θυγατέρα κατὰ τῆς μητρὸς αὐτῆς καὶ νύμφην κατὰ τῆς πενθερᾶς αὐτῆς,}

					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]

					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		\paragraph{Matthew 10:36} % *MATTHEW-10-36 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-36}

			And a man's foes shall be they of his own
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					See \hyperref[MATTHEW-10-25]{Matthew 10:25} for another occurrence of this Greek word, \gr{οἰκιακός}. I'm reminded of \hyperref[DC121-45]{DC 121:45}, and I'm also inclined to take this ``household'' as a figurative thing, speaking of beliefs or habits or other parts of ourselves that move us away from what is good.
					}%
			household.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ἐχθροὶ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου οἱ οἰκιακοὶ αὐτοῦ.}

		\paragraph{Matthew 10:37} % *MATTHEW-10-37 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-37}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[DEUTERONOMY-33-9]{Deuteronomy 33:9--10}.
					}%
			He that loveth father or mother more%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					For some reason I've never keyed in on the fact that it says ``more.'' We ought to be loving our family members as much as we love Christ---I think the restoration helps with this perspective by making those family relationships eternal.
					}
			than me is not worthy of me: and he that loveth son or daughter more than me is not worthy of me.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὁ φιλῶν πατέρα ἢ μητέρα ὑπὲρ ἐμὲ οὐκ ἔστιν μου ἄξιος· καὶ ὁ φιλῶν υἱὸν ἢ θυγατέρα ὑπὲρ ἐμὲ οὐκ ἔστιν μου ἄξιος·}

					% \item[BSV]

			\end{framed}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-38}

			And he that taketh not his cross, and followeth after me, is not worthy of me.%

					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[MATTHEW-16-24]{Matthew 16:24}, \hyperref[MARK-8-34]{Mark 8:34}, \hyperref[MARK-10-21]{Mark 10:21}, \hyperref[LUKE-9-23]{Luke 9:23}, \hyperref[DC56-2]{DC 56:2}, 
					}

			\begin{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:39} % *MATTHEW-10-39 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-39}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					For commentary on this verse, see \hyperref[EXSS-NT-MATTHEW-10-39-SELF]{this extended study}.
					}%
			He that findeth his life shall lose it: and he that loseth his life for my sake shall find it.%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					See especially \hyperref[MATTHEW-16-24]{Matthew 16:24--25}, \hyperref[MARK-8-35]{Mark 8:35}, \hyperref[LUKE-9-24]{Luke 9:24}, \hyperref[MATTHEW-10-38]{Matthew 10:38}, \hyperref[LUKE-17-33]{Luke 17:33}, and \hyperref[JOHN-12-25]{John 12:25}, but also \hyperref[MATTHEW-7-14]{Matthew 7:14}; \hyperref[DC98-13]{DC 98:13} (a useful elaboration of this verse, since it clarifies that the found life is ``eternal life''); \hyperref[DC101-15]{DC 101:15}; \hyperref[DC103-27]{DC 103:27--28}; 
					}

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὁ εὑρὼν τὴν} \hyperref[PSUCHE]{\grl{ψυχὴν}} \gr{αὐτοῦ ἀπολέσει αὐτήν, καὶ ὁ ἀπολέσας τὴν} \hyperref[PSUCHE]{\grl{ψυχὴν}} \gr{αὐτοῦ ἕνεκεν ἐμοῦ εὑρήσει αὐτήν.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					\item[BSV] Qui invenit animam suam perde illam et qui perdiderit animam suam propter me inveniet eam
					\item[ASV] He that findeth his life shall lose it; and he that loseth his life for my sake shall find it.
					\item[AB 1986] One who grasps at self will lose it, but one who rejects self on my account will gain it.
					\item[SPNT] Whoever finds their life will lose it, and whoever loses their life for my sake will find it.
					\item[ESV] Whoever finds his life will lose it, and whoever loses his life for my sake will find it.
					\item[NASB] ``He who has found his life will lose it, and he who has lost his life for My sake will find it.
					\item[NICNT (France)] Anyone who finds their life will lose it, and anyone who loses their life because of me will find it.
					\item[NIRV] If anyone finds his life, he will lose it. If anyone loses his life because of me, he will find it.
					\item[NIV] Whoever finds their life will lose it, and whoever loses their life for my sake will find it.
					\item[NRSV] Those who find their life will lose it, and those who lose their life for my sake will find it.
					\item[WBC 33a PDF 367] The person who `finds' his or her life will lose it, and the person who loses his or her life for my sake will find it.
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 10:40} % *MATTHEW-10-40 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-10-40}

			\footnote{%
				% \label{}%
				Compare \hyperref[DC84-35]{DC 84:35--39}.
				}%
			He that receiveth you receiveth me, and he that receiveth me receiveth him that sent me.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ὁ δεχόμενος ὑμᾶς ἐμὲ δέχεται, καὶ ὁ ἐμὲ δεχόμενος δέχεται τὸν ἀποστείλαντά με.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]

And, according to Jesus, John fulfilled Malachi's prophecy (\hyperref[MALACHI-3-1]{Mal. 3:1}; \hyperref[MALACHI-4-5]{4:5--6}), being the one who would prepare Israel for the `great and terrible day of the LORD' (Mal. 4:5).''

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					A passive form of \gr{βιάζω}. BDAG 154a begins the entry by saying, ``most of this entry concerns probabilities relating to \gr{β.} in Mt 11:12 and par. \hyperref[LUKE-16-16]{Lk 16:16}. The principal semantic problem is whether \gr{β.} is used negatively (`in malam partem') or positively (`in bonam partem'), a problem compounded by the question of the function of these vss. in their literary context. In Gk. lit. \gr{β.} is most often used in the unfavorable sense of attack or forcible constraint.'' So this is an expression that is somewhat difficult in itself to figure out. Compare the use of \gr{βιάζω} in \hyperref[LUKE-16-16]{Luke 16:16}.
					}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 11:16} % *MATTHEW-11-16 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-11-16}

			But whereunto shall I liken this generation?%
			\footnote{%
				% \label{}%
				BIBBC 905: ``As children of all cultures tend to do, Jewish children in Jesus's day imitated adult rituals, playacting certain behavior associated with celebrations (festivals and weddings) and funerals. Music was an important part of both kinds of events: songs of celebration for dancing and songs of lament for grieving (11:17). Jesus compared his generation's opinion of him and John the Baptist (11:18--19) to children who were playing `grown-up,' but it's difficult to determine the assigned roles: who are the children who are playing the instruments (`we played the flute for you') and the children who refused to play the game (`but you didn't dance')? If Jesus and John are the `we,' then Jesus was criticizing the apathy of his generation. Jesus came `eating and drinking,' but the people refused to celebrate Jesus's miracles; John `came neither eating nor drinking,' but the people refused to fast---that is, to get serious about their sin. But if the children reciting the rhyme were the people of `this generation' (which seems more likely in light of vv. 18--19), then the one refusing to dance is John and the one refusing to mourn is Jesus. In other words, the people thought that John had taken repentance too far (11:18: `He has a demon'---as if to say, `He needs to loosen up and have some fun'). And Jesus needed to get serious about sin (9:14: `Why do we and the Pharisees fast often, but your disciples do not fast?'; and 11:19: `Look, a glutton and a drunkard'; cf. \hyperref[DEUTERONOMY-21-20]{Deut. 21:20}---what a father was supposed to say to the people to disown his rebellious son).''
				}
			It is like unto children sitting in the markets, and calling unto their fellows,

			\scriptsize

					\item[SBLG] \gr{Τίνι δὲ ὁμοιώσω τὴν γενεὰν ταύτην; ὁμοία ἐστὶν παιδίοις καθημένοις ἐν ταῖς ἀγοραῖς ἃ προσφωνοῦντα τοῖς ἑτέροις}

				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					ZIBBC NT 01 204: ```Sackcloth and ashes' were familiar symbols of repentance. The rough cloth woven from camel or goat's hair was worn close to the skin as a symbolic rejection of comfort and ease in cases of mourning, national disaster, or repentance (e.g., \hyperref[JONAH-3-5]{Jonah 3:5--8}). Ashes symbolized loss, whether sprinkled over one's head or lying in them \hyperref[ESTHER-4-1]{Est. 4:1--3}).''

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 11:29} % *MATTHEW-11-29 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-11-29}

			Take my yoke%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					TDNT for \gr{ζυγός}: ``In other NT passages [outside of Revelation] where \gr{ζυγός} occurs the sense is that of `yoke.' It is used only figuratively. As in older and contemporary Greek usage, and as also in contemporary Jewish usage, the word expresses a relation of absolute dependence. Though there are differences in detail, the general sense is unmistakably the same...In Mt. 11:29f. Jesus invites the \gr{κοπιῶντες καὶ πεφορτισµένοι} to take upon them His \gr{ζυγός}: \gr{ἄρατε τὸν ζυγόν µου ἐφ᾽ ὑµᾶς καὶ µάθετε ἀπ᾽ ἐµοῦ, ὅτι πραΰς εἰµι καὶ ταπεινὸς τῇ καρδίᾳ, καὶ εὑρήσετε ἀνάπαυσιν ταῖς ψυχαῖς ὑµῶν· ὁ γὰρ ζυγός µου χρηστὸς καὶ τὸ φορτίον µου ἐλαφρόν ἐστιν.} The saying, which formally belongs to the larger context of wisdom sayings, is obviously formulated as a conscious paradox. How can a \gr{ζυγός} be easy? But the paradox evaporates when we remember who is speaking and to whom. Jesus is clearly speaking to those who already bear a \gr{ζυγός}, for He refers expressly to His \gr{ζυγός}, to the \gr{ζυγός} of the Messiah, contrasting this with another \gr{ζυγός}, with the other \gr{ζυγός}. But this other \gr{ζυγός} can only be that of worship under the Law, which involves the oppressive labour and attitude of the slave. This is clear from \hyperref[MATTHEW-23-4]{Mt. 23:4}, where we find the image of the burden used. In this saying, therefore, a contrast is drawn between the Messianic \gr{ζυγός} of Jesus and the \gr{ζυγός} of legalism. Jesus is thus contrasted with the \gr{νόµος}, as in John's Gospel and sometimes in Paul. Thus the \gr{ζυγός} which Jesus lays on those who accept it is the new worship of God in the free access to the Father which is the portion of all who bow obediently to the Word of Jesus and receive from Him the revelation of the will of God. This access is not the result of human attainment; it is the gift of Jesus in His Word and person. For this reason acceptance of His \gr{ζυγός} is possible only in faith in Him as the Christ. Hence only to those who believe in Him is His yoke a \gr{ζυγὸς χρηστός} and His burden a \gr{φορτίον ἐλαφρόν}.''
					}
			upon you, and learn of me; for I am \hyperref[MEEKNESS]{meek} and \hyperref[LOWLINESS]{lowly} in heart: and ye shall find rest unto your souls.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἄρατε τὸν ζυγόν μου ἐφ᾽ ὑμᾶς καὶ μάθετε ἀπ᾽ ἐμοῦ, ὅτι} \hyperref[PRAUS]{\grl{πραΰς}} \gr{εἰμι καὶ} \hyperref[TAPEINOS]{\grl{ταπεινὸς}} \gr{τῇ καρδίᾳ, καὶ εὑρήσετε ἀνάπαυσιν ταῖς} \hyperref[PSUCHE]{\grl{ψυχαῖς}} \gr{ὑμῶν·}

					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---

					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἢ πῶς δύναταί τις εἰσελθεῖν εἰς τὴν οἰκίαν τοῦ ἰσχυροῦ καὶ τὰ σκεύη αὐτοῦ ἁρπάσαι, ἐὰν μὴ πρῶτον δήσῃ τὸν ἰσχυρόν; καὶ τότε τὴν οἰκίαν αὐτοῦ διαρπάσει.}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-12-31}

			Wherefore I say unto you, All manner of sin and blasphemy shall be forgiven unto men: but the blasphemy against the Holy Ghost shall not be forgiven unto men.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{διὰ τοῦτο λέγω ὑμῖν, πᾶσα ἁμαρτία καὶ βλασφημία ἀφεθήσεται τοῖς ἀνθρώποις, ἡ δὲ τοῦ πνεύματος βλασφημία οὐκ ἀφεθήσεται.}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-12-32}

			And whosoever speaketh a word against the Son of man, it shall be forgiven him: but whosoever speaketh against the Holy Ghost, it shall not be forgiven him, neither in this world, neither in the world to come.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ὃς ἐὰν εἴπῃ λόγον κατὰ τοῦ υἱοῦ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου, ἀφεθήσεται αὐτῷ· ὃς δ᾽ ἂν εἴπῃ κατὰ τοῦ πνεύματος τοῦ ἁγίου, οὐκ ἀφεθήσεται αὐτῷ οὔτε ἐν τούτῳ τῷ αἰῶνι οὔτε ἐν τῷ μέλλοντι.}

		% ---

					\item[SBLG] \gr{Ἢ ποιήσατε τὸ δένδρον καλὸν καὶ τὸν καρπὸν αὐτοῦ καλόν, ἢ ποιήσατε τὸ δένδρον σαπρὸν καὶ τὸν καρπὸν αὐτοῦ σαπρόν· ἐκ γὰρ τοῦ καρποῦ τὸ δένδρον γινώσκεται.}

					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὁ ἀγαθὸς ἄνθρωπος ἐκ τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ θησαυροῦ ἐκβάλλει ἀγαθά, καὶ ὁ πονηρὸς ἄνθρωπος ἐκ τοῦ πονηροῦ θησαυροῦ ἐκβάλλει πονηρά.}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-12-36}

			But I say unto you, That every idle word that men shall speak, they shall give account thereof in the day of judgment.
			
			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{λέγω δὲ ὑμῖν ὅτι πᾶν ῥῆμα ἀργὸν ὃ λαλήσουσιν οἱ ἄνθρωποι, ἀποδώσουσιν περὶ αὐτοῦ λόγον ἐν ἡμέρᾳ κρίσεως·}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					\item[AB 153] I tell you that on the day of judgment men will render account for every harmful word they utter,

				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἄνδρες Νινευῖται ἀναστήσονται ἐν τῇ κρίσει μετὰ τῆς γενεᾶς ταύτης καὶ κατακρινοῦσιν αὐτήν· ὅτι μετενόησαν εἰς τὸ κήρυγμα Ἰωνᾶ, καὶ ἰδοὺ πλεῖον Ἰωνᾶ ὧδε.}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 12:42} % *MATTHEW-12-42 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-12-42}

			The queen of the south%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					ZIBBC NT 01 219: ``The Queen of the South is the queen of Sheba (\hyperref[1KINGS-10-1]{1 Kings 10:1--29}). Sheba is most likely the home of the Sabaeans in southwestern Arabia in present-day Yemen. It was known for its strong agricultural base, but even more for being one of the most important trade centers involving Africa, India, and the Mediterranean countries. Solomon's wisdom was so widely renowned that the queen went to question him and found his wisdom to be more than she had anticipated. She and the people of Nineveh had allowed the revelation of God to penetrate to their pagan hearts and so will be God's eternal witnesses against the Jewish religious leaders that they have not opened their heart to Jesus, the preeminent revelation of God.''
					}
			shall rise up in the judgment with this generation, and shall condemn it: for she came from the uttermost parts of the earth to hear the wisdom of Solomon; and, behold, a greater than Solomon is here.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{βασίλισσα νότου ἐγερθήσεται ἐν τῇ κρίσει μετὰ τῆς γενεᾶς ταύτης καὶ κατακρινεῖ αὐτήν· ὅτι ἦλθεν ἐκ τῶν περάτων τῆς γῆς ἀκοῦσαι τὴν σοφίαν Σολομῶνος, καὶ ἰδοὺ πλεῖον Σολομῶνος ὧδε.}

			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 13:12} % *MATTHEW-13-12 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-13-12}

			For whosoever hath, to him shall be given, and he shall have more abundance:%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Augustine comments: ``For everything which does not give out when given away is not yet possessed in the way in which it should be possessed, while it is possessed and not given away. God says, `the man who has will be given more'. He will give to those who have: this means that for those who make generous use of what they have received he will supplement and increase what he has given. One person had five loaves, and another had seven before the loaves began to be distributed to the hungry, but once the distribution had begun, they managed to fill baskets and hampers even after satisfying so many thousands of people'' (DDC I:2).
					}
			but whosoever hath not, from him shall be taken away even that he hath.%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					The principle taught here is one of the most fundamental in all of the \hyperref[EPIST-REVELATION]{epistemology of revelation}.
					}

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὅστις γὰρ ἔχει, δοθήσεται αὐτῷ καὶ περισσευθήσεται· ὅστις δὲ οὐκ ἔχει, καὶ ὃ ἔχει ἀρθήσεται ἀπ᾽ αὐτοῦ.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					\item[BDAG 715a] ...to the one who has (more) will be given, and that person will have a great abundance...
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-13-14}

			And in them is fulfilled the prophecy of Esaias,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					See \hyperref[ISAIAH-6-9]{Isaiah 6:9--10}.
					}
			which saith, By hearing ye shall hear, and shall not understand; and seeing ye shall see, and shall not perceive:

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{καὶ ἀναπληροῦται αὐτοῖς ἡ προφητεία Ἠσαΐου ἡ λέγουσα· Ἀκοῇ ἀκούσετε καὶ οὐ μὴ συνῆτε, καὶ βλέποντες βλέψετε καὶ οὐ μὴ ἴδητε.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]

			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 13:15} % *MATTHEW-13-15 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-13-15}

			For this people's heart is waxed gross,%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					\gr{παχύνω}, BDAG 702a---the entry lists this verse as meaning \#2, ``\textbf{make impervious} (originally to water), \textbf{\textit{make gross, dull}}.'' It says this is a figurative meaning. The literal meaning is \#1, ``\textbf{make fat, well-nourished}.'' This is the same word used in the LXX for \hyperref[ISAIAH-6-10]{Isaiah 6:10}. LSJ \#I.2.b is ``make gross or dull,'' and in the passive, ``to be coarsened''; \#II.1 is ``become thick'' and is used in other sources of the skull, liquids, and other things. I think this is a way of understanding what it means for your heart to be ``hardened,'' as it's spoken of in the BOM, and in particular it helps us understand what's going on in \hyperref[2NEPHI-33-1]{2 Nephi 33:1}, where the Holy Ghost carries a message ``unto the hearts of the children of men,'' rather than ``into.'' Your heart has to be in a place to understand and receive and internalize. This is an important aspect of the relation between revelation and emotion.
					}
			and their ears are dull of hearing, and their eyes they have closed; lest at any time they should see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and should understand with their heart, and should be converted, and I should heal them.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{ἐπαχύνθη γὰρ ἡ καρδία τοῦ λαοῦ τούτου, καὶ τοῖς ὠσὶν βαρέως ἤκουσαν, καὶ τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς αὐτῶν ἐκάμμυσαν· μήποτε ἴδωσιν τοῖς ὀφθαλμοῖς καὶ τοῖς ὠσὶν ἀκούσωσιν καὶ τῇ καρδίᾳ συνῶσιν καὶ ἐπιστρέψωσιν, καὶ ἰάσομαι αὐτούς.}
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[BSV]
					% \item[ASV]
					% \item[AB ]
					% \item[SPNT]
					% \item[NTR1]
					% \item[NTR2]
					% \item[NTX*]
					% \item[NTXX*]
					% \item[NTXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXX*]
					% \item[NTXXXXXX*]
				\end{compactdesc}
			\end{framed}

		% ---
		\paragraph{Matthew 13:19} % *MATTHEW-13-19 
		% ---
			\label{MATTHEW-13-19}

			When any one heareth the word of the kingdom, and understandeth%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[DC68-25]{DC 68:25} and notes there.
					}
			it not, then cometh the wicked one, and catcheth away%
				\footnote{%
					% \label{}%
					Compare \hyperref[DC93-39]{DC 93:39}.
					}
			that which was sown in his heart. This is he which received seed by the way side.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{παντὸς ἀκούοντος τὸν λόγον τῆς βασιλείας καὶ μὴ συνιέντος, ἔρχεται ὁ πονηρὸς καὶ ἁρπάζει τὸ ἐσπαρμένον ἐν τῇ καρδίᾳ αὐτοῦ· οὗτός ἐστιν ὁ παρὰ τὴν ὁδὸν σπαρείς.}

		\paragraph{Matthew 13:20} % *MATTHEW-13-20 
		% ---

			But he that received the seed into stony places, the same is he that heareth the word, and anon with joy receiveth it;

					\item[SBLG] \gr{ὁ δὲ ἐπὶ τὰ πετρώδη σπαρείς, οὗτός ἐστιν ὁ τὸν λόγον ἀκούων καὶ εὐθὺς μετὰ χαρᾶς λαμβάνων αὐτόν,}

		% ---

			Yet hath he not root in himself, but dureth for a while: for when tribulation or persecution ariseth because of the word, by and by he is offended.

			\begin{framed}
			\scriptsize
				\begin{compactdesc}
					\item[SBLG] \gr{οὐκ ἔχει δὲ ῥίζαν ἐν ἑαυτῷ ἀλλὰ πρόσκαιρός ἐστιν, γενομένης δὲ θλίψεως ἢ διωγμοῦ διὰ τὸν λόγον εὐθὺς} \hyperref[SKANDALIZO]{\grl{σκανδαλίζεται}}\gr{.}
